7. Workspaces, Domain Engines & Collaboration
This section defines the Workspace layer of OS Dashboard AI Assistant: the domain-specialized “rooms” in which users,
personas, and daemons operate over the shared substrate of:
• Domain & Knowledge Model (Projects, Tasks, CIR, Capsules, Policies, Twins) – Section 3
• Planes (Data, Control, Governance) – Section 2
• Driver Fabric & System Execution Layer – Section 5
• Cognitive Agents & Daemons – Section 4
• Data & Storage Architecture – Section 6
Workspaces provide:
• Focused domain engines (Dev, Research, Writer, Cyber, Finance, SRE, Archive, Twin).
• Curated Capsule catalogs and driver scopes.
• Domain-appropriate views, metrics, and collaboration patterns.
• A consistent way to map user intent → persona reasoning → Capsules & drivers → Ledger & Evidence Packs.
7.0 Purpose & Scope
Purpose
Workspaces are domain-specialized surfaces over a shared Project and data graph. They exist to:
• Give each role (developer, researcher, writer, operator, CISO, CFO, etc.) a coherent cockpit.
• Bind drivers, Capsules, policies, and personas into domain-appropriate patterns.
• Enable cross-workspace orchestration while preserving a single canonical state for each Project.
Scope
This section defines:
• The Workspace abstraction and common contracts.
• Each major Domain Engine and its bindings to drivers, Capsules, daemons, and planes.
• The collaboration model for multi-user projects and cross-tenant federation.
• How low-level primitives (System Execution, Package & Env drivers, Capsules, Evidence Packs, sandboxes) are surfaced
in each workspace.
7.1 Workspace Abstraction & Common Contracts
7.1.1 Concept & Responsibilities
A Workspace is a scoped, domain-specific view over:
• A subset of the Project graph (e.g., “Dev aspect of Project X,” “Research aspect of Twin Y”).
• The same underlying CIR, Ledger, Capsule, Blueprint, Twin, and Policy data.
• A curated tooling profile (drivers, Capsules, daemons, personas) tuned to a domain.
Each workspace is responsible for:
• Presenting relevant panels, metrics, and actions for its domain.
• Translating user/persona intents into domain-specific plans and Capsule graphs.
• Emitting structured events into the Project Ledger and, when needed, Evidence Packs.
• Respecting policy scopes, driver allowlists, and residency constraints.
7.1.2 Common Components
• Workspace Descriptor
○ Declares, per workspace type:
§ workspace_id, display name, domain (Dev, Research, Writer, Cyber, etc.).
§ Supported project types (RepoProject, ResearchProject, BookProject, TwinProject, etc.).
§ Supported panels/views (Timeline, Experiments, Pipelines, Risk, Health, Financials, etc.).

• Emitting structured events into the Project Ledger and, when needed, Evidence Packs.
• Respecting policy scopes, driver allowlists, and residency constraints.
7.1.2 Common Components
• Workspace Descriptor
○ Declares, per workspace type:
§ workspace_id, display name, domain (Dev, Research, Writer, Cyber, etc.).
§ Supported project types (RepoProject, ResearchProject, BookProject, TwinProject, etc.).
§ Supported panels/views (Timeline, Experiments, Pipelines, Risk, Health, Financials, etc.).
§ Default personas & daemons (e.g., Sora in Master Stack, Research persona in Research, Cyber Guardian in
Security, Aria in Writer).
§ Bound driver capabilities (Git, CI, MATLAB, systemctl, ffmpeg, CAD tools, finance APIs, etc.).
§ Supported Capsule classes (Dev Capsules, Research Capsules, Security Capsules, Finance Capsules, Twin
Capsules, etc.).
§ Default policy overlays (min logging, baseline safety harnesses).
• Workspace Controller
○ Mediates between UX and the cognitive/control planes:
§ Receives intents scoped to the workspace (e.g., “analyze this PR,” “design a sweep,” “generate quarterly
report”).
§ Assembles domain-specific context bundles (CIR, Ledger slices, env state, metrics).
§ Invokes relevant personas (Sora, Researcher persona, Aria, Cyber Guardian, AIC) and TRF where needed.
§ Produces plans (Capsule graphs + driver invocations) and updates views.
• Workspace Policies
○ Per-workspace overlays on the Governance Plane:
§ Driver scopes (which OS/Unix/Package/Data/SaaS drivers are usable).
§ Risk ceilings (e.g., Dev may restart dev services; Cyber may not run arbitrary curl).
§ Logging/Evidence requirements (e.g., Cyber and SRE require richer Evidence Packs).
• Workspace Capsule Catalog
○ Curated set of Capsules and Capsule Packs enabled in this workspace:
§ Merge Capsules for Dev.
§ Experiment Capsules for Research.
§ Creator Capsules for Writer.
§ Security and Auto-Remediation Capsules for Cyber/SRE.
§ Twin Capsules for Digital Twin workspace.
7.1.3 Interfaces & Contracts
• Workspace → Application Layer
○ Registration and configuration of workspace types.
○ “Handle intent” interface:
§ (intent_type, payload, context) → plan, effects, view_updates
○ View provisioning:
§ get_workspace_view(view_id, filters) → structured data for UI.
• Workspace → Domain Model
○ Scoped read/write access to:
§ Projects, Tasks, CIR docs/blocks, Capsules, Env Blueprints, Twins, Policies.
○ Workspace may create only domain-appropriate entities:
§ Dev workspace creates Dev Tasks, code-adjacent CIR, Dev Capsules.
§ Research workspace creates Experiments, Model Capsules, Hypothesis entries.
§ Writer workspace creates narrative CIR, Canon entries, Publishing Capsules.
• Workspace → Driver Fabric
○ The Workspace Descriptor enumerates a driver scope:
§ e.g., Dev: Git, CI, Unix, Package/Env, container drivers.
§ Research: MATLAB/Python/R drivers, cluster drivers, DB/search drivers.
§ Cyber: security tools (nmap, ss, iptables), log analyzers.
§ Writer: pandoc, LaTeX, ffmpeg, doc APIs.
○ Planner uses this scope to propose executable steps.
7.1.4 Data Flows
• Within a workspace
○ User/persona intent → Workspace Controller → context assembly → cognitive plan → Governance evaluation →
driver calls/Capsule runs → Ledger events → workspace views.
• Across workspaces
○ Events from one workspace become signals in others:
§ Research results surface as prompts in Writer (for papers).

○ Planner uses this scope to propose executable steps.
7.1.4 Data Flows
• Within a workspace
○ User/persona intent → Workspace Controller → context assembly → cognitive plan → Governance evaluation →
driver calls/Capsule runs → Ledger events → workspace views.
• Across workspaces
○ Events from one workspace become signals in others:
§ Research results surface as prompts in Writer (for papers).
§ Security findings surface as blockers in Dev and Master Stack.
§ Financial impacts surface as risk/priority adjustments in Master Stack.
§ Env drift from SRE surfaces in Dev and Research as red flags.
7.1.5 Failure Modes & Recovery
• Missing driver or permission
○ Workspace drops into degraded mode:
§ Read-only views; actions disabled or restricted.
§ Remediation tasks automatically created (e.g., “Install MATLAB Capsule,” “Connect Jira,” “Configure Git
host”).
• Policy conflicts
○ When requested actions are denied:
§ Workspace surfaces explicit, human-readable policy errors (“Security Policy X disallows package Y; request
override?”).
§ Offers alternative compliant paths (e.g., sandbox, limited scope, or manual approval).
• Stale or inconsistent context
○ Workspaces rely on Ledger timestamps and Project Intelligence to detect staleness and:
§ Prompt for refresh.
§ Or request re-computation from source workspaces/daemons.
7.1.6 Security / Compliance Notes
• Workspaces are not security boundaries by themselves.
○ Boundaries are defined by tenant, role, policy, classification, and driver scopes.
• Each workspace type defines a minimum viable policy profile:
○ Cyber & SRE workspaces require richer logging and Evidence Packs.
○ Finance workspace requires stricter classification and residency enforcement.
• All workspace actions are mediated by Governance Plane:
○ No workspace can bypass policies or driver constraints.
7.2 Master Stack & Project Management Engine
7.2.1 Purpose & Responsibilities
The Master Stack is the global project nerve center:
• Maintains a prioritized, multi-dimensional view over all projects and tasks.
• Integrates signals from all workspaces (Dev, Research, Writer, Finance, Cyber, SRE, Twin).
• Correlates abstract project status with real world state:
○ Env Blueprint convergence.
○ CI/health metrics.
○ Security posture.
○ Financial and resource constraints.
7.2.2 Key Components
• ProjectGraphEngine
○ Models dependencies between projects/sub-projects:
§ e.g., “Simulation Engine v2 must ship before OS Dashboard Rewrite X.”
○ Stores cross-workspace constraints (Dev → Research → Docs → SRE → Audit).
• Task & MasterStackScheduler
○ Aggregates Tasks from all workspaces into a unified Master Stack queue.
○ Prioritization heuristics incorporate:
§ Strategic importance / impact.
§ Financial constraints (runway, cashflow).
§ Security risk & compliance urgency.
§ Capacity & availability (human + compute).
§ Project Intelligence metrics (momentum, risk hotspots).
• EnvironmentAwareProjectView
○ For each project:
§ Env Blueprint status (converged / drifted / unknown).
§ Health of critical jobs and services (CI, backup, ETL, periodic jobs) via SRE workspace.

§ Financial constraints (runway, cashflow).
§ Security risk & compliance urgency.
§ Capacity & availability (human + compute).
§ Project Intelligence metrics (momentum, risk hotspots).
• EnvironmentAwareProjectView
○ For each project:
§ Env Blueprint status (converged / drifted / unknown).
§ Health of critical jobs and services (CI, backup, ETL, periodic jobs) via SRE workspace.
§ Security posture summary via Cyber workspace.
§ Cost and model usage via Billing & Usage Fabric.
7.2.3 Interfaces & Data Flows
• Inputs from workspaces & subsystems
○ Dev: code health, merge risk, CI status, tech debt.
○ Research: experiment progress, model validity, simulation risk.
○ Writer: drafting progress, publication pipeline status.
○ Cyber: risk scores, open vulnerabilities, pending hardening tasks.
○ SRE: error budgets, env drift, latency/SLO breaches.
○ Finance: ROI projections, runway, cost anomalies.
○ Project Intelligence: derived metrics per project (Section 4.5).
• Outputs
○ Global stack views:
§ “What matters this week,” “Runway-critical,” “Security-critical,” “Research-critical,” etc.
○ Suggested Re-focus Plans:
§ Reordering tasks, deferring non-critical work, accelerating critical flows.
○ API surfaces for daemons (OracleDaemon, Billing Optimizer, CriticDaemon) and personas (Sora).
• Example Flow
○ Intent: “Refocus on OS Dashboard monetization.”
§ Sora + Master Stack:
□ Re-rank tasks across Dev, Research, Writer, Finance.
□ Identify dependencies and environment changes.
□ Propose a Re-focus Capsule plan subject to Governance.
7.2.4 Failure Modes & Recovery
• Mis-prioritization
○ AIC + OracleDaemon backtest decisions vs actual outcomes and:
§ Propose adjustments.
§ Flag systematically biased heuristics.
○ Human override always possible:
§ Overrides recorded in Ledger as explicit “PriorityOverride” events.
• Stale workspace signals
○ Detect via timestamps and health heartbeats.
○ Trigger refresh flows or degrade reliability of certain signals (with visible indicators).
7.2.5 Security / Compliance Notes
• Master Stack is role-filtered:
○ Different roles (IC, team lead, C-suite, auditor) see different scopes and details.
• High-impact re-prioritizations (e.g., de-prioritizing security or compliance tasks) can:
○ Require approval.
○ Be blocked by policy.
7.3 Dev & DevOps Workspace – Code & Change Intelligence
The Dev Workspace is the domain engine for software development and delivery: code, environment, CI/CD, and runtime
behavior.
7.3.1 Purpose & Responsibilities
• Provide an AI-augmented environment for:
○ Code authoring, review, and refactoring.
○ Merge analysis and risk assessment.
○ Commit→Task generation.
○ Dev env bootstrap & drift repair.
○ CI/CD orchestration & auto-remediation.
• Integrate:
○ Git + CI + System Execution + Package & Env + Observability + Marketplace Capsules.
7.3.2 Code Merge Advisor
Responsibilities

○ Merge analysis and risk assessment.
○ Commit→Task generation.
○ Dev env bootstrap & drift repair.
○ CI/CD orchestration & auto-remediation.
• Integrate:
○ Git + CI + System Execution + Package & Env + Observability + Marketplace Capsules.
7.3.2 Code Merge Advisor
Responsibilities
• Analyze merge candidates (branches/PRs) to:
○ Identify conflicts and risky changes.
○ Estimate impact across services/components.
○ Recommend merge strategies under project priorities (stability vs speed vs performance).
Key Components
• MergeContextCollector
○ Gathers:
§ Diffs, commit history, author intent.
§ Associated tasks and CIR docs.
§ CI results and runtime metrics.
• PriorityProfileStore
○ Encodes repo/project preferences:
§ No breaking APIs without version bump & Evidence Pack.
§ Always run specific test suites before merging.
§ Avoid merging when error budget is nearly exhausted.
• AdvisorEngine
○ Uses model reasoning + TRF + AIC where appropriate to:
§ Suggest resolution per file or region.
§ Suggest extra tests or Canary Capsules.
§ Produce human-readable Merge Risk Report.
Driver & Capsule Integration
• Git Driver + System Execution:
○ git status/log/diff/merge via governed CLI.
• CI/CD Drivers:
○ Trigger pre- and post-merge pipelines.
• Capsules:
○ Merge Capsules encode standard flows (e.g., rebase + run tests + update changelog + generate Evidence Pack).
Failure Modes
• Uncertain or high-risk merges:
○ Advisor marks as “non-auto-mergeable.”
○ Requires human review with annotated suggestions.
• Policy restrictions (e.g., branch protection):
○ Prevent auto-merge; Advisor only proposes patch/plan.
7.3.3 Commit → Task Generator
Responsibilities
• Transform code diffs into structured Tasks:
○ Add tests, update docs, refactor, cleanup, infra changes, env updates, security fixes.
Data Flows
• On commit or PR creation:
○ Git hook / Ledger event triggers analysis.
○ Commit diff + CIR representation + Master Stack context → Task suggestions:
§ Tagged by code region, risk, and dependencies.
Outputs
• Dev Tasks:
○ Task items created in Project/Dev workspace, linked to commits and code regions.
• Capsule suggestions:
○ Automated refactoring or migration Capsules where patterns are recognized.

Responsibilities
• Ensure dev machines and CI agents are aligned with Environment Blueprints.
Key Features
• Blueprint derivation:

○ Task items created in Project/Dev workspace, linked to commits and code regions.
• Capsule suggestions:
○ Automated refactoring or migration Capsules where patterns are recognized.
7.3.4 Dev Environment Automation via Package & Env Drivers
Responsibilities
• Ensure dev machines and CI agents are aligned with Environment Blueprints.
Key Features
• Blueprint derivation:
○ From known-good dev machines or CI images.
• “Run This Project Here” wizard:
○ Compares current machine to Blueprint; generates a convergence plan using Package & Env drivers.

○ Developer- or team-specific env presets (editors, CLIs, tools), sharable as Capsules.

• All environment changes:
○ go through Package & Env drivers (Section 5.4) and emit Ledger PACKAGE_CHANGE / ENV_DIFF events.

• Trigger and track:

○ Each stage can be encoded as a Capsule step graph.
• Feed results into:

○ Operator Workspace (for reliability),
○ Record Auditor (for release evidence).

○ Subject to Safety Harnesses and policy.

7.5 Writer Workspace
7.5.1 Writer Workstation Engine (Theory → Narrative, Lore → Canon)

Capabilities

consistent style constraints.

Capabilities

Responsibilities
• Maintain canonical definitions and lore for:
○ Fictional universes (worlds, factions, histories, magic/tech systems).

○ Entities, timelines, relationships, locations, items, rules, constraints.

7.5.3 Narrative Guidance Engine (Draft-to-Completion Co-Author)
Purpose
• Act as a continuous, context-aware co-author that guides a work from initial idea to finished manuscript.
Responsibilities
• Interpret high-level briefs, outlines, and thematic goals into concrete writing plans.
• Provide moment-to-moment narrative suggestions while preserving:
○ Voice and tone constraints (Aria-aligned stylistic guardrails).
○ Structural and canonical constraints (AIC-aligned logical guardrails).
• Maintain a “narrative state machine” that tracks:
○ Open arcs, unresolved setups, foreshadowed elements.
○ Emotional beats, tension curves, and pacing patterns.
Capabilities
• Draft-from-outline:
○ Convert structured outlines into first-pass drafts, respecting canon links and research references.
• Beat-level guidance:
○ Suggest next paragraph / scene / section based on target emotion, tension, or information goals.
• Gap analysis:
○ Identify missing transitions, under-explained concepts, or dropped threads.
• Multi-path exploration:
○ Generate alternative scene variants or structural options (branching drafts) while preserving canonical
consistency.
• Integration with Story QA:
○ Consume QA findings as constraints (“strengthen payoff for Setup X”, “clarify rule Y earlier”) and feed them
back into rewrite suggestions.

• Continuity engine:
○ Track entities across the manuscript (who is where, when, with what state).
○ Detect impossible movements, contradictory states, or missing connective scenes.

8. Capsule System, Project Ledger & Workflow Synthesis / Automation
This section defines the execution grammar of OS Dashboard – how work is encoded, governed, executed, and
remembered:
• Capsule System – executable, inspectable, shareable workflows.
• Project Ledger – tamper-evident, queryable history of actions, environments, and decisions.
• Workflow Synthesis & Automation – recording real activity (UI + CLI + driver calls) and turning it into reusable
Capsules and apps.
Capsules + Ledger sit at the intersection of all three planes:
• Data Plane – code, parameters, artifacts, results, and evidence.
• Control Plane – orchestration of drivers, tasks, and workflows.
• Governance Plane – policies, safety harnesses, approvals, auditability.
They are also the primary substrate for higher tiers (Meta-Stack, Reality Twin Mesh, Self-Evolving Capsule Ecosystem, etc.),
which operate over encoded workflows, twins, and histories.
8.1 Capsule Taxonomy (Template, Analytical, Executable, Infra, Composite, Test)
8.1.1 Purpose / Responsibilities
Define a minimal but expressive set of Capsule kinds so that:
• Planning, governance, and marketplace behavior can specialize by type.
• Users, personas, and daemons can reason about what a Capsule is allowed to do and what it is expected to produce.
• Risk, review, and sandboxing policies can be tuned per type.

They are also the primary substrate for higher tiers (Meta-Stack, Reality Twin Mesh, Self-Evolving Capsule Ecosystem, etc.),
which operate over encoded workflows, twins, and histories.
8.1 Capsule Taxonomy (Template, Analytical, Executable, Infra, Composite, Test)
8.1.1 Purpose / Responsibilities
Define a minimal but expressive set of Capsule kinds so that:
• Planning, governance, and marketplace behavior can specialize by type.
• Users, personas, and daemons can reason about what a Capsule is allowed to do and what it is expected to produce.
• Risk, review, and sandboxing policies can be tuned per type.
8.1.2 Core Capsule Types
1. Template Capsules
○ Non- or lightly-executable scaffolds.
○ Capture reusable patterns: task frames, workflow skeletons, doc structures.
○ Examples: “Standard PR Review Flow”, “Experiment Design Template”, “Narrative Outline Template”.
○ Typically instantiated into Executable/Analytical Capsules per project.
2. Analytical Capsules
○ Read-heavy analytics, simulation, and reporting workflows.
○ Use Data / Research / Simulation / SaaS drivers and System Execution in read mode.
○ Outputs: dashboards, reports, derived datasets, CIR documents, metrics.
○ Side-effects limited to new artifacts and CIR updates (no infra writes).
3. Executable Capsules
○ General automation with write side-effects.
○ May invoke OS, Unix, Package & Env, SaaS, Data, and Workflow drivers.
○ Used for: builds, migrations, reconciliations, content generation, routine operations.
○ Require full policy evaluation, sandboxing as appropriate, and complete audit logging.
4. Infra Capsules
○ Specialized subclass of Executable Capsules with explicit infra-changing semantics:
§ Deploy services, roll back releases, patch servers, rotate secrets, modify firewall rules, scale clusters.
○ Strongest safety, approval, and Evidence Pack requirements.
○ Typically run from Operator, SRE, or Cyber workspaces.
5. Composite Capsules
○ “Capsule-of-Capsules” (meta-Capsules).
○ Compose other Capsules into graphs:
§ Sequential, parallel, conditional, event-driven, sagas with compensating steps.
○ Side-effects occur only via constituent Capsules.
○ Primary vehicle for cross-workspace orchestration.
6. Test Capsules
○ Validation flows that assert properties of systems, models, environments, or other Capsules.
○ Examples: unit/integration tests, smoke tests, env/driver self-checks, compliance tests.
○ Often paired 1:N with Executable/Infra Capsules (pre- and post-conditions).
8.1.3 Governance & Marketplace Integration
• Each Capsule declares a single primary type plus optional domain tags (security, finance, research, etc.).
• Governance and Marketplace use type + tags to determine:
○ Required review steps and approvers.
○ Allowed scopes (e.g., Infra Capsules disabled in certain tenants/environments).
○ Default sandbox level and policy packs.
8.2 Capsule Structure, Metadata, Tests & Constraints
8.2.1 Purpose
Define a canonical structure for Capsules so the platform can:
• Analyze and govern them statically.
• Execute them deterministically.
• Render and edit them across tools.
• Integrate them with Ledger, Evidence Packs, and Meta-Stack.
8.2.2 Core Structure (Conceptual Schema)
Every Capsule version includes at least:
• Identity & Classification
○ capsule_id (stable UUID), version (semver), primary type (8.1).
○ Name, description, domain tags (Dev, Research, Writer, Finance, Cyber, SRE, Twin, etc.).
○ Owner(s), tenant, creation/update timestamps.
○ Visibility scope: private, project, team, tenant, marketplace.
• Interface Definition
○ Inputs: parameters with types, defaults, validation constraints, and hints (e.g., “path to dataset”, “env name”).
○ Outputs: result types (CIR docs, artifacts, metrics, status objects), plus primary artifacts for indexing.
○ Pre-conditions: predicates over environment, data, or state that must be true before running.

○ capsule_id (stable UUID), version (semver), primary type (8.1).
○ Name, description, domain tags (Dev, Research, Writer, Finance, Cyber, SRE, Twin, etc.).
○ Owner(s), tenant, creation/update timestamps.
○ Visibility scope: private, project, team, tenant, marketplace.
• Interface Definition
○ Inputs: parameters with types, defaults, validation constraints, and hints (e.g., “path to dataset”, “env name”).
○ Outputs: result types (CIR docs, artifacts, metrics, status objects), plus primary artifacts for indexing.
○ Pre-conditions: predicates over environment, data, or state that must be true before running.
○ Post-conditions: invariants expected after completion (e.g., tests passing, health checks green).
• Execution Plan Stub
○ High-level step graph (DAG): steps refer to driver capabilities and/or sub-Capsules.
○ No raw free-form shell inside the manifest:
§ Shell/CLI logic is wrapped through driver declarations (e.g., unix.exec with structured arguments).
• Environment & Dependencies
○ Reference to one or more Environment Specs / Blueprints (8.3, 8.12).
○ Required driver capabilities (Git, DB, MATLAB, CAD, CI/CD, etc.).
○ Supported OS families and constraints.
• Tests & Checks
○ Linked Test Capsules for:
§ Unit, integration, regression, env sanity.
○ Embedded checks:
§ Post-run assertions (e.g., “CI pipeline green”, “no 5xx in last N minutes”).
• Governance Metadata
○ Risk tier (read-only, write, infra-admin).
○ Required approver roles and quorum (e.g., Security Reviewer + SRE Lead).
○ Required policy packs (e.g., “Prod Infra Ops”, “PII-Safe Reporting”).
○ Evidence Pack requirements and templates (8.17).
• Observability Hooks
○ Metrics to emit.
○ Logging level and redaction rules (PII/PHI/IP, classification).
○ Trace correlation behavior (span/link to Ledger events).
8.2.3 Constraints
• Capsule manifests are declarative and statically analyzable:
○ Drivers, potential resources, and approximate risk can be inferred before runtime.
• Imperative logic lives inside:
○ Driver implementations, scripts referenced via driver wrappers, or sub-Capsules.
• A Capsule must be validatable against:
○ Schema, dependency resolution, policy packs, and environment specs.
8.3 Capsule Manifests, Environment Specs & Driver Dependencies
8.3.1 Capsule Manifest
The Capsule Manifest is the machine-readable bundle that:
• Encodes the Capsule structure (8.2).
• References:
○ Environment Blueprints.
○ Driver capabilities and external services.
○ Policy packs and risk tier.
• Serves as:
○ The artifact used by Execution Engine to run Capsules.
○ The unit of versioning in Ledger and Marketplace.
8.3.2 Environment Specs / Blueprints
• Per-Capsule or per-Project Environments (see also 3.9, 5.4, 7.10, 8.12):
○ Runtimes (Python/Node/Java/MATLAB/R/Julia/… + versions).
○ CLIs (git, ffmpeg, kubectl, tar, etc.).
○ Libraries (pip/conda/npm/brew/choco/apt/dnf/… packages).
○ System requirements (GPU, RAM, disk, OS distro).
○ Env vars and config fragments.
• OS-aware mappings:
○ macOS → Homebrew.
○ Windows → Chocolatey/Winget.
○ Linux → apt/dnf/yum/pacman/zypper.

○ CLIs (git, ffmpeg, kubectl, tar, etc.).
○ Libraries (pip/conda/npm/brew/choco/apt/dnf/… packages).
○ System requirements (GPU, RAM, disk, OS distro).
○ Env vars and config fragments.
• OS-aware mappings:
○ macOS → Homebrew.
○ Windows → Chocolatey/Winget.
○ Linux → apt/dnf/yum/pacman/zypper.
• Used by:
○ “Run This Project Here” (7.12.7).
○ Blueprint & convergence flows (8.12).
○ Health & drift monitoring (8.16).
8.3.3 Driver Dependencies
• Capsules declare capabilities, not bare binaries, where possible:
○ e.g., video.transcode, db.query, ci.run_pipeline.
• For low-level integrated flows, they can require specific tools and versions:
○ e.g., kubectl >= 1.29, ffmpeg >= 5.0.
• Dependencies categorized:
○ Hard dependencies (required).
○ Soft dependencies (optional features).
8.3.4 Contracts
• Planning Engine uses manifests to:
○ Check driver availability and compatibility.
○ Check policy/packs and environment readiness.
• Package & Env drivers use specs to:
○ Converge hosts/sandboxes to needed environments.
• Ledger records:
○ Manifest version and Blueprint ID used for each run.
8.4 Capsule Lifecycle (Design → Draft → Review → Publish → Run → Deprecate)
8.4.1 Stages
1. Design
○ Capsule authored via text editor or Operator Studio (8.19) or synthesized from recordings (8.11).
○ Structure may be incomplete; no guarantees.
2. Draft
○ Manifest structurally valid; dependencies resolved.
○ Runnable only in:
§ Developer/test sandboxes, or
§ Designated “labs” tenants/workspaces.
○ Metrics gathered to inform review.
3. Review
○ Static checks:
§ Safety, security, complexity, env coverage.
○ Human review:
§ Domain expert, SRE, Security, Finance, etc. as required by policy.
○ Test runs:
§ Test Capsules executed in representative environments.
4. Publish
○ All required checks/tests passed, approvals granted.
○ Capsule marked ACTIVE within visibility scope (project/team/tenant/marketplace).
○ Version pinned and immutable.
5. Run
○ Executions recorded as Run instances:
§ Bound to manifest version, Blueprint version, policy version.
○ Each run emits:
§ Ledger events, logs, metrics, traces.
§ Optional Evidence Packs (8.17).
6. Deprecate & Retire
○ Deprecate:
§ Capsule hidden from default discovery, flagged in search.
§ New use discouraged; existing flows may keep using pinned version until migrated.
○ Retire:
§ New runs prohibited.

§ Ledger events, logs, metrics, traces.
§ Optional Evidence Packs (8.17).
6. Deprecate & Retire
○ Deprecate:
§ Capsule hidden from default discovery, flagged in search.
§ New use discouraged; existing flows may keep using pinned version until migrated.
○ Retire:
§ New runs prohibited.
§ Historical data kept for audit and replay.
8.4.2 Governance Integration
• Lifecycle transitions drive Ledger events (capsule.created, capsule.review_started, capsule.published,
capsule.deprecated, etc.).
• Policies may:
○ Require dual-control approvals for Infra/Security Capsules.
○ Limit publishing rights to specific roles.
8.5 Capsule Execution Engine & Sandboxing (Driver Scopes, Budgets, Policies)
8.5.1 Responsibilities
• Interpret Capsule manifests and orchestrate:
○ Step graphs across drivers, Capsules, and tasks.
• Enforce:
○ Policies, safety harnesses, budgets, and resource limits.
• Ensure:
○ Deterministic, observable, auditable runs.
8.5.2 Key Components
• Execution Planner
○ Resolves Capsule step graph → execution DAG.
○ Assigns step placement (local node, sandbox, cluster, remote host).
○ Deals with parallelization and ordering.
• Sandbox Manager
○ Creates per-run containers/VMs/sandboxes as needed (8.15).
○ Applies:
§ Resource limits (CPU, RAM, disk).
§ Network policies (egress controls, whitelisted endpoints).
§ Filesystem scoping (project workspaces, readonly mounts).
• Policy & Safety Layer
○ Evaluates Capsule + parameters + caller context against:
§ Policy packs (Section 10).
§ Safety Harnesses (e.g., command allow/block lists, path guards).
○ Can:
§ Require approvals.
§ Force sandbox level.
§ Reject or downgrade high-risk steps.
• Budget & Quota Enforcer
○ Ties into Billing & Usage Fabric (Section 15).
○ Enforces:
§ Model token budgets.
§ Driver call quotas.
§ System Execution quotas (command count, total runtime).
§ Cluster/compute quotas.
• Run Recorder
○ Emits structured events to Ledger for:
§ Run start/end.
§ Step attempts.
§ Policy decisions.
§ Errors and retried steps.
8.5.3 Execution Semantics
• Idempotent vs non-idempotent steps explicitly declared.
• Failure handling:
○ Retries with backoff where allowed.
○ Fallback or compensation flows (saga pattern) for multi-step operations.

§ Step attempts.
§ Policy decisions.
§ Errors and retried steps.
8.5.3 Execution Semantics
• Idempotent vs non-idempotent steps explicitly declared.
• Failure handling:
○ Retries with backoff where allowed.
○ Fallback or compensation flows (saga pattern) for multi-step operations.
• Human-in-the-loop:
○ Approval nodes for critical steps (e.g., production deploy, secret rotation).
8.6 Capsule–Driver, Capsule–Workspace & Capsule–Twin Dependencies
8.6.1 Capsule ↔ Driver
• Explicit dependency graph:
○ Used for impact analysis when:
§ Drivers are upgraded, removed, or policy-restricted.
• Driver capabilities referenced symbolically:
○ e.g., db.query, ci.run, simulation.run, hpc.submit_job.
• Driver changes can:
○ Trigger Capsule re-validation and review workflows.
8.6.2 Capsule ↔ Workspace
• Capsules are often workspace-scoped (7.x):
○ Dev Capsules in Dev Workspace,
○ Research Capsules in Research Workspace,
○ Security Capsules in Cyber Workspace, etc.
• Some Capsules are cross-workspace:
○ E.g., “End-to-End Release”, “Monthly Close”, “Audit Bundle”.
8.6.3 Capsule ↔ Twin
• Digital Twin & Enterprise Twin workspaces (7.11) use Capsules as:
○ Simulation steps (run model X with parameters Y).
○ Control steps (adjust system/twin settings).
○ Observation steps (snapshot metrics, logs, env state).
• Twin dependencies:
○ Tracked as part of Capsule metadata and Ledger events for later simulation/backtesting.
8.7 Project Ledger Model (Events, Hash Chain, Causal Graph)
8.7.1 Purpose
Provide an append-only, tamper-evident, time-ordered record of:
• Human actions, persona/daemon actions, driver calls, Capsule runs, environment changes, policy decisions, and twin
updates.
8.7.2 Core Model
• Ledger Event
○ event_id, project_id, tenant_id.
○ timestamp.
○ actor_type (user, daemon, persona, Capsule).
○ actor_id.
○ workspace, capsule_id (optional), twin_id (optional).
○ event_type (e.g., CAPSULE_RUN_STARTED, DRIVER_INVOKED, ENV_DIFFERENCE, POLICY_DECISION,
MODEL_EVALUATED, etc.).
○ Structured payload (JSON/CBOR).
○ prev_hash, event_hash for hash chain (per project and optionally per tenant).
• Causal Graph Layer
○ parent_event_ids and/or caused_by fields.
○ correlation_id for grouping by workflow/incident/release.
○ Used to reconstruct causal chains.
8.7.3 Integrity & Querying
• Integrity:
○ Hash chains validated periodically by Ledger Integrity Daemon.
○ Optional anchoring to tenant-level or external roots for high-assurance tenants.
• Queries:
○ Filter by project, time range, actor, event type, capsule, workspace, or correlation.
○ Underpin:
§ Record Auditor & Logbook Workspace (7.9).

8.7.3 Integrity & Querying
• Integrity:
○ Hash chains validated periodically by Ledger Integrity Daemon.
○ Optional anchoring to tenant-level or external roots for high-assurance tenants.
• Queries:
○ Filter by project, time range, actor, event type, capsule, workspace, or correlation.
○ Underpin:
§ Record Auditor & Logbook Workspace (7.9).
§ Regulatory views.
§ Temporal backtesting and twin replay.
8.8 Lineage, Replay, Time-Travel & Environment Reconstruction
8.8.1 Lineage
• For any artifact (CIR doc, model, dataset, twin state, binary):
○ Trace back:
§ Capsule runs and steps that produced it.
§ Input artifacts and parameters.
§ Environment blueprints and versions.
§ Policy/approval context.
• Represented as graph objects referencing Ledger events, Capsules, and CIR blocks.
8.8.2 Replay
• Modes:
○ Logical replay:
§ Walk through historical events and reconstruct state transitions.
○ Execution replay:
§ Re-run Capsule with pinned manifests and environment specs in sandbox.
• Used for:
○ Debugging and regression analysis.
○ Forensics and legal investigations.
○ Scientific reproducibility.
8.8.3 Time-Travel
• UIs and APIs to reconstruct project or twin as of T:
○ CIR versions,
○ Capsule graph versions,
○ Environment state (via Ledger + Blueprints),
○ Policies in effect.
• Integrated with Archive, Continuity & Resonance Workspace (7.6).
8.8.4 Environment Reconstruction
• Combine:
○ Env events from Ledger (PACKAGE_CHANGE, ENV_SNAPSHOT),
○ Blueprints,
○ Machine profiles (Local vs Enterprise).
• Generate:
○ Convergence plans (for current hosts),
○ Sandbox definitions (for historical reenactment).
8.9 Knowledge Mining & Resonance over Capsules, CIR, Ledger & Research Graphs
8.9.1 Purpose
Treat Capsules + Ledger + CIR + Research Knowledge Graph (7.4.7) as first-class knowledge objects for:
• Discovering patterns.
• Identifying best practices and anti-patterns.
• Driving Self-Evolving Capsule Ecosystem (8.23).
• Feeding Meta-Stack engines.
8.9.2 Capabilities
• Pattern mining:
○ Frequently co-invoked Capsules.
○ Canonical “golden paths” (e.g., release flows that yield fewer incidents).
• Anti-pattern detection:
○ Capsule versions correlated with incidents, drift, or failures.
• Cross-domain resonance:
○ Recognize structural patterns shared between domains (e.g., research pipelines vs financial scenarios vs infra
maintenance).
• Recommendations:

• Pattern mining:
○ Frequently co-invoked Capsules.
○ Canonical “golden paths” (e.g., release flows that yield fewer incidents).
• Anti-pattern detection:
○ Capsule versions correlated with incidents, drift, or failures.
• Cross-domain resonance:
○ Recognize structural patterns shared between domains (e.g., research pipelines vs financial scenarios vs infra
maintenance).
• Recommendations:
○ Suggest composite Capsules, template packs, or refactors.
○ Surface dormant workflows from Archive/Resonance (7.6) that match current tasks.
8.10 Workflow Engine & Orchestration Semantics (Tasks, Drivers, Capsules)
8.10.1 Workflow Model
• Workflows are expressed as Capsule graphs over nodes:
○ Capsule nodes (automation).
○ Driver nodes (direct driver calls when Capsule abstraction is overkill).
○ Task/human nodes (manual work, approvals).
○ Control nodes (conditionals, fan-in/fan-out, retries, compensations).
• Edges encode:
○ Control flow (ordering, branch conditions, failure paths).
○ Data flow (outputs mapped to downstream inputs).
○ Causality (used by Ledger for correlation).
8.10.2 Execution Semantics
• Deterministic given:
○ Same inputs, Capsule versions, Blueprints, and policies (modulo external systems).
• Support:
○ Parallelism and synchronization.
○ Retries with strategies (fixed, exponential, capped).
○ Sagas and compensating actions for multi-step workflows.
8.10.3 Integration with Tasks & Master Stack
• Workflows can:
○ Generate tasks (e.g., manual review, physical steps).
○ Complete or update tasks upon success/failure.
• Master Stack (7.2):
○ Uses workflow status to compute project progress and risk.
8.11 Workflow → App Synthesizer (Record / Macro → Script → Capsule → App)
8.11.1 Purpose
Allow users to “do the work once” and let the system convert it into reusable, governed automation.
8.11.2 Flow
1. Record
○ OS/UI drivers record UI actions (clicks, keystrokes, file picks).
○ Unix Execution layer wraps and records terminal commands.
○ File system drivers observe file operations.
○ SaaS/Workflow drivers log high-level interactions.
2. Synthesize
○ AIC/TRF + specialized planners:
§ Segment stable workflow steps vs noise.
§ Identify parameters (paths, thresholds, names) vs constants.
§ Map raw shell to driver operations (CLI→API wrappers, 8.14).
3. Script Graph
○ Build a structured workflow definition:
§ Steps, inputs, outputs.
§ Control flow and basic error handling.
§ Environment requirements.
4. Capsule Packaging
○ Wrap script into a Draft Capsule:
§ Manifest, tests (where possible), safety constraints.
§ Default policy bindings (personal scope, low-risk).
○ Attach UI metadata for a simple “app”:
§ Form fields, toggles, run button.
5. Review & Promotion
○ For personal use:

4. Capsule Packaging
○ Wrap script into a Draft Capsule:
§ Manifest, tests (where possible), safety constraints.
§ Default policy bindings (personal scope, low-risk).
○ Attach UI metadata for a simple “app”:
§ Form fields, toggles, run button.
5. Review & Promotion
○ For personal use:
§ May remain Draft with relaxed rules in Local Mode.
○ For shared or high-risk use:
§ Must pass Review/Publish lifecycle (8.4) before appearing in team/tenant catalogs.
8.12 Environment Blueprints & Rebuild / Convergence Flows
8.12.1 Blueprint Model
• Named, versioned, first-class objects:
○ Tools, packages, configs, env vars, runtime constraints.
○ Optional attached Capsules (e.g., “post-convergence smoke tests”).
• Scoped at:
○ Project level (Dev, Research, Finance, Cyber).
○ Role level (backend dev, quant, SRE).
○ Machine profile level (laptop, CI runner, cluster node).
8.12.2 Convergence Engine
• Compares current state vs Blueprint:
○ Uses Package & Env drivers and system inspection.
• Produces change plan:
○ Installs/upgrades/removes packages.
○ Applies config changes.
○ Suggests manual steps (where automation not possible).
• Executes change plan under policy:
○ Some operations may require approvals.
○ All steps recorded in Ledger.
8.12.3 Usage
• “Run This Project Here” (7.12.7).
• Capsule pre-run convergence (for strict reproducibility).
• Fleet-level standardization in Enterprise Mode.
8.13 Auto-Remediation Playbooks & “Fix-It Buttons” (SRE, Infra, Security, Dev)
8.13.1 Purpose
Encode operational runbooks as Capsules that can be:
• Triggered automatically by alerts.
• Invoked manually via “Fix-It” buttons in Operator, SRE, and Cyber workspaces.
8.13.2 Characteristics
• Typically Infra or Executable Capsules.
• Steps include:
○ Diagnostics (logs, metrics, traces).
○ Remediation actions (restart, rollback, reconfigure, converge env).
○ Verification (Test Capsules, smoke checks).
• Triggers:
○ Observability alerts (Section 6.6).
○ Drift detection (8.16).
○ Security findings (7.7).
8.13.3 Governance
• Auto-run vs manual-run policies controlled by risk tier and environment (dev/stage/prod).
• All runs produce Evidence Packs for incidents and postmortems.

8.16 Health & Drift Monitoring (Capsules, Workflows, Blueprints, Environments)

○ Bottlenecks, frequent manual overrides, repeated retries.

○ Missing tools, repeated package failures, security misconfigs.

○ Self-Evolving Capsule Ecosystem (8.23),
○ Master Stack (7.2) for risk prioritization.

8.23 Self-Evolving Capsule Ecosystem (Meta-Improvement Loop)
8.23.1 Purpose
Continuously improve the Capsule ecosystem using evidence from Ledger, observability, and outcomes – under strict
governance.
8.23.2 Mechanisms
• Collect signals:
○ Usage frequency, success/failure rates, cost profiles, user ratings, health metrics.
• Analyze:
○ Detect underperforming Capsules.
○ Identify high-performing patterns.
• Propose:
○ Parameter changes, step reordering, partial refactors.
○ New Composite Capsules from frequently co-invoked capsules.
○ Consolidation or deprecation of duplicates.
8.23.3 Safety
• Proposals result in Draft Capsule versions or alternatives.
• No automatic promotion to Publish:
○ Must pass the standard lifecycle (8.4) with appropriate approvals.
• Particularly constrained in regulated tenants:
○ Evolution may be advice-only.
8.24 Capsule Governance, Versioning, Approvals & Review Workflows
8.24.1 Governance Model
• Policy Engine enforces:
○ Who can design, modify, publish, run, or deprecate Capsules.
• Rights and constraints linked to:
○ Role, workspace, risk tier, tenant, classification, environment (dev/stage/prod).
8.24.2 Versioning
• Semantic versioning recommended:
○ Major: behavior/contract changes or increased risk.
○ Minor/Patch: internal improvements, bug fixes.
• Historical versions:
○ Immutable; always available for replay, audit, and rollback.
8.24.3 Approvals & Review
• Multi-step review:
○ Functional review, security review, SRE/Performance review, domain review.
• Policy-driven requirements:
○ Higher risk tiers require more reviewers, dual-control, or regulator review.

• Forking:
○ Create variants for teams/tenants or experimental branches.
• Merging:
○ Structural diff of Capsule manifests and graphs.
○ Merge flows analogous to code review.
8.25.3 Federation
• In cross-tenant setups and HyperMesh:
○ Capsules can be shared as read-only or forkable artifacts.
○ Ownership, IP, and revenue-sharing handled via Marketplace and Governance layers (Section 9 & 15).

9. Extensibility, Plugins, Driver Packs, Marketplace & Cross-OS Mesh
Purpose of Section 9
This section defines how OS Dashboard is extended, distributed, and federated:
• How plugins, drivers, Capsules, packs, and vertical “OS editions” are authored and installed.
• How the Marketplace and private catalogs curate, price, and govern those extensions.
• How knowledge and execution flow across OS instances (Inter-OS Knowledge Network, HyperMesh, HyperSymphony).
• How these mechanisms align with Governance & Policy (Section 10), Data & Ledger (Sections 6 & 8), and Economics
& Billing (Section 15).
Everything that extends the system must:
• Express itself through drivers, Capsules, and manifests.
• Run under the Governance Plane (Policy Engine, Safety Harness, Billing, Ledger).
• Be portable between Local Mode and Enterprise Mode.
9.1 Extensibility Principles, Safety Constraints & Trust Model
Purpose
Define the rules by which internal and external contributors extend OS Dashboard without compromising safety,
governance, or coherence.
Core Principles
1. Driver-first abstraction
○ All external systems (apps, CLIs, APIs, infra, data) are surfaced as drivers with manifests.
○ Plugins and Capsules orchestrate drivers; they never bypass the Driver Fabric with ad-hoc network or OS calls.
2. Manifest-driven, policy-aware
○ Every extension (driver, plugin, Capsule, pack, edition) ships with a declarative manifest:
§ Capabilities, dependencies, environment specs.
§ Risk classification and required policy packs.
§ Pricing and licensing metadata (when applicable).
○ Policy Engine and Billing Fabric consume manifests for enforcement and metering.
3. Least privilege & scoped access
○ Extensions run with explicitly declared data scopes, driver scopes, network scopes, and budget scopes.
○ The Governance Plane enforces scopes at runtime; no “ambient authority”.
4. Multi-tier trust
○ Trust tiers determine default permissions, review depth, and installability:
§ Core / Tier-0 – first-party, bundled with platform.
§ Verified / Tier-1 – certified third-party (deep review + tests).
§ Marketplace / Tier-2 – reviewed but not fully certified (restricted scopes).
§ Local / Tier-3 – personal/org-internal, dev/experimental (heavily sandboxed, tenant-scoped).
○ Tenants and regulators can further restrict allowed tiers.
5. Stable contracts, evolvable internals
○ Public SDKs, driver contracts, and manifest schemas are strongly versioned.
○ Extensions may evolve internally, but breaking contract changes require new major versions and migration paths.

§ Core / Tier-0 – first-party, bundled with platform.
§ Verified / Tier-1 – certified third-party (deep review + tests).
§ Marketplace / Tier-2 – reviewed but not fully certified (restricted scopes).
§ Local / Tier-3 – personal/org-internal, dev/experimental (heavily sandboxed, tenant-scoped).
○ Tenants and regulators can further restrict allowed tiers.
5. Stable contracts, evolvable internals
○ Public SDKs, driver contracts, and manifest schemas are strongly versioned.
○ Extensions may evolve internally, but breaking contract changes require new major versions and migration paths.
6. Offline-aware
○ Local Mode must support offline installation from signed bundles.
○ Enterprise tenants may run fully private app stores, optionally mirroring a subset of the public Marketplace.
9.2 Plugin Runtime & Sandbox Model (Permissions, Scopes, Isolation)
Purpose
Provide a controlled execution environment for code extensions (plugins) while forcing all side-effects through governed
drivers.
Plugin Types
• UI plugins – panels, visualizers, custom workspace views, dashboard widgets.
• Logic plugins – pure computation, formatting, domain rules, light transformations.
• Integration plugins – thin shims around external APIs; conceptually realized as drivers.
Execution Model
• Out-of-process by default:
○ Plugins run in separate processes or containers per tenant or per extension.
○ All OS, filesystem, network, and driver access goes through the platform’s Execution Proxy.
• In-process plugins:
○ Restricted to pure computation and formatting (no I/O or external calls).
○ Primarily for UI composition and minor logic.
Permissions & Scopes
Each plugin manifest declares:
• Data scopes – which tenants/projects/classes of CIR it may read/write.
• Driver scopes – which drivers it may call and in what modes (read-only / read-write / admin).
• Command scopes – allowed Unix/OS commands (mapped via Safety Harness rules).
• Network scopes – allowed outbound endpoints (if any) and protocols.
• Budget scopes – max runtime, CPU/GPU, LLM tokens, driver invocation counts per call.
At runtime:
• The Permission Broker checks requested actions against:
○ Declared scopes,
○ Tenant policies,
○ Safety Harnesses,
○ Budgets & cost guardrails.
Sandbox Guarantees
• Resource quotas (CPU, memory, I/O).
• Timeouts and circuit breakers per invocation.
• Isolated filesystem (per-plugin working dir + restricted project mounts).
• Restricted or no network, depending on trust tier and policy.
• No persistent state except via domain APIs (CIR, Ledger, Capsule Store, Observability, etc.).
Failure Modes & Recovery
• Plugin crash:
○ Sandbox terminated; error logged to Ledger and Observability.
○ Host degrades gracefully (feature disabled, fallback UI).
• Misbehaving plugin (timeouts, error spikes, resource abuse):
○ Automatically rate-limited, then disabled by Policy Engine.
• Policy violation:
○ Call fails with structured error; Record Auditor logs denied action for later review.
Security / Compliance Notes
• All plugin→driver calls go through the same Policy Engine, Billing Fabric, and Ledger as core features.
• Enterprise policies can restrict:
○ Allowed plugin languages, runtimes, dependency sources.
○ Use of networked runtimes vs fully local sandboxes.
9.3 Plugin, Capsule & Driver Pack SDKs & Scaffolding
Purpose
Provide opinionated SDKs so extensions are easy to build and hard to mis-build.

Security / Compliance Notes
• All plugin→driver calls go through the same Policy Engine, Billing Fabric, and Ledger as core features.
• Enterprise policies can restrict:
○ Allowed plugin languages, runtimes, dependency sources.
○ Use of networked runtimes vs fully local sandboxes.
9.3 Plugin, Capsule & Driver Pack SDKs & Scaffolding
Purpose
Provide opinionated SDKs so extensions are easy to build and hard to mis-build.
Plugin SDK
• Language bindings (initially TypeScript/JS and Python; others pluggable).
• Helpers for:
○ Declaring manifests (capabilities, scopes, env requirements).
○ Calling drivers via a typed driver client API.
○ Invoking the Model Layer (under policy).
○ Emitting Ledger events, metrics, traces.
• Local dev server / simulator:
○ Stubbed OS Dashboard instance with fake drivers and policies for rapid iteration.
Capsule Authoring SDK
• Schema helpers for Capsule definitions:
○ Inputs/outputs, step graphs, driver dependencies, environment specs, tests, governance metadata.
• Integrated with:
○ Workflow→App Synthesizer (8.11) – capture flows, then refine and harden.
○ Operator Studio (8.19) – visual graph editing and export to Capsule manifest.
• Validation tools:
○ Static analysis for dangerous patterns (unsafe shell, overly broad scopes).
○ Policy simulation for different policy packs (e.g., dev vs prod vs regulated).
Driver Pack / Pack SDK
• Templates for:
○ Driver Packs – logical bundles of drivers.
○ Capsule Packs – curated Capsule sets + blueprints.
○ Vertical Editions – profession OS bundles (drivers + Capsules + workspace presets).
• Manifest schemas for:
○ Included driver IDs / Capsule IDs.
○ Policy pack pre-requisites.
○ Pricing metadata and trust tier.
Scaffolding CLI
• osdash create plugin
• osdash create capsule
• osdash create driver-pack
• osdash create capsule-pack
• Pre-wired:
○ Example tests,
○ CI templates,
○ Linting & manifest validators.
9.4 Driver SDK & Distribution Channels
Purpose
Enable creation of consistent, governable drivers for OS, SaaS, infra, data, and research systems.
Driver SDK
Drivers declare:
• Type – OS / Unix / Package & Env / SaaS / Data / Research / Hardware / Governance.
• Capabilities – fine-grained actions (e.g., filesystem.read, git.diff, ticket.create, simulation.run, metric.read).
• I/O Schemas – structured input and output types.
• Idempotency & side-effects – whether operations are safe to retry or destructive.
• Error semantics – error classes and retry hints.
• Environment requirements – libraries, binaries, OS constraints, endpoints.
• Observability hooks – metrics, logs, trace spans.
The SDK provides:
• Contract tests (capability coverage, failure behavior, timeouts).
• Policy simulation hooks (what happens if invoked under various policy packs).
• SBOM generation and vulnerability scanning integration.
Distribution Channels

• Error semantics – error classes and retry hints.
• Environment requirements – libraries, binaries, OS constraints, endpoints.
• Observability hooks – metrics, logs, trace spans.
The SDK provides:
• Contract tests (capability coverage, failure behavior, timeouts).
• Policy simulation hooks (what happens if invoked under various policy packs).
• SBOM generation and vulnerability scanning integration.
Distribution Channels
• Core drivers – bundled with OS Dashboard runtime; updated with platform.
• Org drivers – internal integrations distributed via Enterprise App Store (9.9).
• Marketplace drivers – public drivers published via Marketplace; subject to certification and trust tiering.
9.5 Publishing Flow (Validation, Testing, Certification, Versioning)
Purpose
Define how plugins, drivers, Capsules, and packs move from local dev to tenant catalogs and public Marketplace.
Stages
1. Local / Draft
○ Built with SDK; manifests may be incomplete.
○ Runs only in dev mode or personal Local Mode.
2. Team / Workspace Scope
○ Shared within a project or team.
○ Basic validations enforced:
§ Manifest schema, dependency resolution, sanity tests.
3. Tenant / Enterprise Scope
○ Available across the tenant.
○ Requires:
§ Passing automated tests (unit/integration).
§ Static analysis and dependency scanning (SBOM, CVEs).
§ Policy simulations (scopes, safety harness use, blast radius).
4. Marketplace Candidate
○ Submitted to Marketplace:
§ Deep validation runs (load tests, misuse scenarios).
§ Optional manual review (security, domain) for high-risk categories.
○ Assigned a proposed trust tier.
5. Certified / Published
○ Receives trust tier (Core / Verified / Marketplace / Community).
○ Version, compatibility matrix, and licensing terms recorded.
○ Listed in public catalog and/or Enterprise catalog.
Versioning & Deprecation
• Semantic versioning recommended; multiple versions may coexist.
• Deprecation:
○ Announced window.
○ Migration hints or migration Capsules where possible.
○ Historical versions retained for replay and audit.
Failure & Remediation
• Validation failure:
○ Extension remains local/private; diagnostic reports provided.
• Security incident or severe bug:
○ Extension quarantined or forcibly disabled.
○ Remediation Capsules may be orchestrated to clean up usage (e.g., rotate credentials, revert changes).
9.6 Executable Capsules & Marketplace Core Design
Purpose
Define how Capsules and Packs are distributed, discovered, priced, and run via the Marketplace.
Marketplace Placement
• Sits above the Driver Registry and Capsule Store.
• Distributes manifests and packs (drivers, Capsules, OS editions); runtime always executes via local Driver Registry &
Capsule Runtime – never directly from Marketplace.
Key Concepts
• Capsule Artifact – versioned bundle:
○ Capsule spec (structure, steps, driver calls, policies).
○ Environment specs (Blueprints).
○ Test suite, sample inputs/outputs, Evidence Pack templates.
• Listing – Marketplace entry:

• Distributes manifests and packs (drivers, Capsules, OS editions); runtime always executes via local Driver Registry &
Capsule Runtime – never directly from Marketplace.
Key Concepts
• Capsule Artifact – versioned bundle:
○ Capsule spec (structure, steps, driver calls, policies).
○ Environment specs (Blueprints).
○ Test suite, sample inputs/outputs, Evidence Pack templates.
• Listing – Marketplace entry:
○ Metadata, category, tags, trust tier, pricing model.
○ Required Driver Packs, Capsule Packs, Policy Packs.
○ Compatibility and residency constraints.
Invocation & Governance
• At install time:
○ Tenant policies determine whether listing is allowed.
○ Compatibility checks with drivers, OS version, and compliance packs.
• At run time:
○ Capsule Runtime (8.5) enforces:
§ Policy, sandbox, budgets, safety harnesses.
○ Billing Fabric meters usage according to listing’s pricing contract.
Failure Modes
• Marketplace unavailable:
○ Already-installed Capsules/Drivers continue working.
○ New installs/updates blocked until recovery.
• Corrupt or malicious manifests:
○ Block publication/installation; alerts raised for security review.
9.7 Capsule Template Packs & Vertical Bundles
Purpose
Package curated Capsule sets + Blueprints for domain-specific workflows as installable “Capsule Packs”.
Components
• A set of Capsule IDs (workflows, templates, labs).
• References to:
○ Required Driver Packs.
○ Required Environment Blueprints.
• Optional:
○ Workspace presets (panels, dashboards).
○ Default policy templates.
Example Packs
• Video & Media Pack – encoding, thumbnailing, asset pipelines.
• Writer & Publishing Pack – drafting, formatting, compilation, release pipelines.
• Quant & Finance Pack – ingestion, analytics, backtesting, risk reporting.
• Data Science & ML Pack – data wrangling, training, evaluation, deployment.
• SRE & Sysadmin Pack – health checks, backups, restart flows, incident runbooks.
• Research & Lab Pack – experiment design, simulation, analysis, and reporting.
Each pack declares:
• Required policy packs (e.g., policy.finance.v1, policy.healthcare.v1).
• Risk profile (local only vs infra-touching vs regulated data-touching).
9.8 Driver Packs & Vertical Editions (Finance, Cyber, Research, Creator, Quant, etc.)
Purpose
Group drivers, Capsules, policies, and workspace layouts into vertical editions (“X OS”).
Driver Packs
• Logical bundles referencing:
○ Driver Registry IDs only (no code in the pack itself).
• Include:
○ Default Capsule Packs (9.7).
○ Blueprint suggestions (toolchains).
○ Policy templates (baseline governance).
○ Observability dashboards.
Vertical Editions (“Profession OS”)
Built from:
• One or more Driver Packs,
• Capsule Template Packs,

• Include:
○ Default Capsule Packs (9.7).
○ Blueprint suggestions (toolchains).
○ Policy templates (baseline governance).
○ Observability dashboards.
Vertical Editions (“Profession OS”)
Built from:
• One or more Driver Packs,
• Capsule Template Packs,
• Workspace presets and personas,
• Policy & compliance packs.
Examples:
• Creator OS – Writer Workspace + Media Pack + backup & DR Capsules.
• Quant OS – Research Workspace + Quant & Finance packs + HPC drivers.
• DevOps OS – Dev Workspace + SRE Pack + Infra Capsules.
• Cyber OS – Cybersecurity Workspace + security scanners + hardening Capsules.
Tenants can subscribe to Editions, which:
• Activate relevant drivers and packs.
• Apply policy templates and billing models.
• Expose curated dashboards and workflows.
9.9 Enterprise App Store / Private Marketplace Modes
Purpose
Enable tenants (especially enterprises and regulated orgs) to control which extensions exist inside their environment.
Modes
• Private-only
○ Catalog contains only internal drivers, plugins, Capsules, and packs.
○ Public Marketplace is disabled or air-gapped snapshots are imported manually.
• Hybrid
○ Public Marketplace visible through:
§ Tenant allowlists/denylists by category, publisher, trust tier.
○ Internal extensions co-exist and may override or wrap public ones.
• Air-gapped
○ No direct internet access.
○ Marketplace snapshots exported/imported as signed offline bundles.
○ Only Core drivers, internal extensions, and manually imported certified items available.
Governance
• Tenant admins define:
○ Allowed trust tiers and categories.
○ Extra approvals for specific risk tiers (e.g., infra, finance, PHI).
○ Retention and audit policies for install/update histories (stored in Ledger).
9.10 Curation, Ranking, Reputation & Marketplace Curator Daemons
Purpose
Maintain a high-quality, safe, and discoverable ecosystem at scale.
Signals
• Installation, retention, uninstallation rates.
• Capsule run success/failure rates, error types, and blast radius.
• Performance (latency, resource usage per run).
• Security posture:
○ Vulnerabilities, incident involvement, policy violation frequency.
• User feedback:
○ Ratings, reviews, complaint patterns.
• Evidence quality:
○ Presence and completeness of Evidence Packs for high-stakes flows.
Marketplace Curator Daemons
• AIC-backed daemons that:
○ Recompute rankings and recommendations periodically.
○ Flag low-performing or risky extensions for deprecation or re-review.
○ Suggest new Capsule Packs or Editions based on observed usage clusters.
○ Provide per-tenant tuned recommendations (subject to tenant policy and privacy).
Reputation Model
• Each extension and publisher has:

Marketplace Curator Daemons
• AIC-backed daemons that:
○ Recompute rankings and recommendations periodically.
○ Flag low-performing or risky extensions for deprecation or re-review.
○ Suggest new Capsule Packs or Editions based on observed usage clusters.
○ Provide per-tenant tuned recommendations (subject to tenant policy and privacy).
Reputation Model
• Each extension and publisher has:
○ Per-tenant reputation,
○ Global reputation (where allowed by policy).
• Tenants may override:
○ Block specific publishers.
○ Boost internal or partner content.
9.11 Third-Party Risk Management & Review Processes
Purpose
Manage the security and compliance risks introduced by third-party extensions.
Processes
• Automated Checks
○ Static analysis for insecure patterns.
○ SBOM + dependency vulnerability scanning (CVE tracking).
○ Policy simulation:
§ What can this extension do in various policy configurations?
• Manual Review (high-risk)
○ For drivers and Capsules that:
§ Access infra admin APIs,
§ Manipulate regulated financial/health data,
§ Modify security posture (firewalls, IAM, keys),
§ Provide regulatory automation (e.g., legal/regulatory filings).
○ Security, compliance, and legal sign-off as required.
• Risk Tiering
○ “View-only”, “Local-only”, “Test-only”, “Prod-touching”, “Regulated-data-touching”.
○ Tenant policies and compliance packs can:
§ Restrict use of certain tiers.
§ Force manual approval or sandboxing.
Remediation
• if an extension is found malicious or dangerously flawed:
○ Immediately quarantined; further installs blocked.
○ Existing installations disabled or sandbox-restricted.
○ Tenants alerted via dashboards and notifications.
○ Optional remediation Capsules run (e.g., rotating credentials, reverting configs).
9.12 Economic Model for Drivers, Packs, Capsules & Features
Purpose
Align Marketplace incentives with platform and tenant economics (details in Section 15) without polluting runtime
semantics.
Pricing Surfaces
• Drivers & Driver Packs
○ Per-seat, per-call, per-throughput-unit (requests, rows, GB).
• Capsules & Capsule Packs
○ Per-run, per-task, per-pipeline, or subscription models.
• Vertical Editions / Profession OS
○ Tiered subscriptions (Basic/Pro/Enterprise),
○ Possibly blended with resource-based pricing.
• Extras
○ Training/curriculum packs,
○ Research packs,
○ Twin packs.
Revenue Share
• For Marketplace items, revenue may be shared between:
○ Platform operator,
○ Extension provider(s),
○ Optionally referrers or resellers.
Integration with Billing Fabric

○ Training/curriculum packs,
○ Research packs,
○ Twin packs.
Revenue Share
• For Marketplace items, revenue may be shared between:
○ Platform operator,
○ Extension provider(s),
○ Optionally referrers or resellers.
Integration with Billing Fabric
• Every extension usage event (driver call, Capsule run, sandbox spawn, env convergence) emits:
○ Tenant ID, extension ID, publisher ID, resource units (tokens, CPU seconds, GB, etc.), SKU reference.
• Billing Fabric:
○ Aggregates usage,
○ Applies pricing contracts,
○ Produces invoices and revenue share statements.
9.13 Inter-OS Knowledge Network & Pattern / Template Sharing
Purpose
Enable OS Dashboard instances to share patterns and templates without sharing raw data.
Concept
• A knowledge-only network between instances:
○ Shares Capsule templates, twin schemas, environment Blueprints, curricula, and best-practice patterns.
○ Aggregates anonymized statistics from Ledgers:
§ Success rates, failure patterns, performance envelopes.
Mechanics
• Instances publish:
○ Canonical Capsule / workflow signatures (structure, not data).
○ Abstracted input/output shapes and metrics (ranges, distributions).
• Other instances:
○ Discover and import these as templates.
○ Adapt them locally with their own data, drivers, and policies.
Governance
• Participation is opt-in per tenant and per jurisdiction.
• Strict guarantees:
○ No raw CIR, logs, or proprietary artifacts leave an instance.
○ Anonymization and aggregation techniques are documented, auditable, and subject to Regulator Fabric review.
9.14 Enterprise & Civilization Knowledge Market (Capsules, Twins, Packs, IP)
Purpose
Elevate Marketplace to a global market for executable knowledge: Capsules, Twins, Research Packs, IP bundles.
Assets
• Capsule Libraries – vetted workflows for industries (e.g., regulatory reporting, sector-specific analytics).
• Digital Twin Templates – reusable twin definitions for plants, networks, markets, architectures.
• Research Packs – reproducible experiment setups (code, environment specs, data lineage, Evidence Packs).
• Curriculum Packs – lab scenarios, training flows, teaching Capsules built on real workflows.
Contracts & Licensing
• Each asset declares:
○ License terms (commercial, academic, consortium, F/OSS compatible).
○ IP ownership & provenance (backed by Ledger lineage).
○ Allowed regions, industries, and constraints.
Integration
• Built on Marketplace substrate:
○ Same publishing, pricing, and trust tiers.
○ Stronger emphasis on Evidence Packs and provenance (Section 8.17).
• Used heavily by:
○ Meta-Stack components (Strategy Garden, Enterprise Twin, Civilization Knowledge systems).
9.15 HyperMesh – Global / Cross-Org Capsule Execution Fabric
Purpose
Enable governed cross-org execution of Capsules across multiple OS Dashboard instances.
Concept
• Each instance exposes a controlled subset of Capsules/drivers as Mesh-exported capabilities.
• Other instances can call these capabilities as remote Capsule steps without direct data access.
Architecture

○ Meta-Stack components (Strategy Garden, Enterprise Twin, Civilization Knowledge systems).
9.15 HyperMesh – Global / Cross-Org Capsule Execution Fabric
Purpose
Enable governed cross-org execution of Capsules across multiple OS Dashboard instances.
Concept
• Each instance exposes a controlled subset of Capsules/drivers as Mesh-exported capabilities.
• Other instances can call these capabilities as remote Capsule steps without direct data access.
Architecture
• Mesh Gateway per tenant:
○ Authenticates incoming/outgoing cross-org calls.
○ Performs policy checks, rate limiting, and logging.
• Federation Protocol:
○ Capability discovery (what remote Capsules/drivers are offered).
○ Contract negotiation (data shapes, policies, SLAs, pricing).
○ Secure invocation & response.
Execution Model
• A Capsule graph may contain:
○ Local steps → executed locally.
○ Remote steps → executed via HyperMesh on partner instances.
• Both caller and callee:
○ Log their own Ledger events with shared correlation IDs.
○ Enforce their own policies and budgets.
Security & Compliance
• Default-deny:
○ No cross-org execution unless explicitly configured and authorized.
• Strong end-to-end encryption, signatures, and mutual attestation.
• Regulator view:
○ Cross-org executions can be inspected with Evidence Packs and Ledger slices from each participant.
9.16 HyperSymphony – Cross-Org Revenue-Sharing Workflow Orchestrator
Purpose
Layer economic orchestration on top of HyperMesh for multi-party Capsule workflows.
Concept
• A Symphony Graph is a Capsule graph spanning multiple tenants/orgs:
○ Each step owned and executed by a different party.
○ Revenue sharing agreements baked into the graph manifest.
Mechanics
• Each step declares:
○ Pricing policy (per run, per unit, success-fee basis, or hybrid).
○ Participant identities and shares (or formulas tied to metrics).
• Billing Fabric:
○ Aggregates cross-org usage,
○ Applies revenue-sharing rules,
○ Produces settlements for each org.
Governance
• Symphony manifests are:
○ Signed by participating orgs.
○ Subject to Law-of-the-OS / AI Court constructs (Section 10, Section 17) for dispute resolution.
• Evidence Packs:
○ Provide tamper-evident proof of who did what, when, with which Capsule versions.
9.17 Strategy Garden & Autonomous Capsule Fund
Purpose
Treat Capsules, Capsule graphs, and Packs as strategic assets in a portfolio.
Concept
• Strategy Garden:
○ Workspace for exploring, evaluating, and prioritizing Capsule graphs (e.g., “Security Automation Transformation”,
“Monetization Engine”, “Creator OS Rollout”).
• Autonomous Capsule Fund:
○ Engine that allocates budgets (compute, attention, cash) to Capsule development and rollout based on:
§ Usage, impact, ROI, and strategic fit.
Integration
• Draws signals from:
○ Marketplace metrics (installs, ratings, retention).

• Strategy Garden:
○ Workspace for exploring, evaluating, and prioritizing Capsule graphs (e.g., “Security Automation Transformation”,
“Monetization Engine”, “Creator OS Rollout”).
• Autonomous Capsule Fund:
○ Engine that allocates budgets (compute, attention, cash) to Capsule development and rollout based on:
§ Usage, impact, ROI, and strategic fit.
Integration
• Draws signals from:
○ Marketplace metrics (installs, ratings, retention).
○ Billing & Usage (revenue generated, cost consumed).
○ Governance & policy (where automation is allowed).
• Feeds:
○ Meta-Stack strategy engines (Section 17) to prioritize which Capsule ecosystems to grow, refactor, or retire.
9.18 Connectors & Integrations – Overview & Contracts
Purpose
Define how external systems are onboarded as drivers/connectors and surfaced through Marketplace and Packs.
Categories & Contracts
1. Productivity Suites & Content Systems
○ Office suites, Google Workspace, CMSes.
○ Contracts:
§ Ingest content into CIR (documents, emails, calendars).
§ Write-back flows (sync changes made in OS Dashboard).
§ Event triggers (new doc, updated issue).
2. Code Hosts, CI/CD, DevOps Tooling
○ GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket, Azure DevOps, Jenkins, GitHub Actions, etc.
○ Contracts:
§ Repos, branches, PRs, pipelines, artifacts, logs.
§ Trigger builds/tests, check statuses, annotate PRs.
3. Finance, Accounting, Banking Systems
○ Accounting tools, ERPs, banking APIs, tax engines.
○ Contracts:
§ Statements, ledgers, invoices, journal entries.
§ Write-back for reconciliations and reporting.
4. Research Data Sources & Publication Databases
○ arXiv, PubMed, patent databases, proprietary data vendors.
○ Contracts:
§ Search, ingest metadata, link PDFs into CIR & Research Graph.
5. Legacy, Mainframe & Terminal Systems
○ Terminal UIs, mainframes, old ERP/CRM.
○ Contracts:
§ Stable command/transaction abstractions over UI/terminal flows using Terminal & UI drivers.
6. Cloud Providers, Infra APIs & Edge Platforms
○ AWS, Azure, GCP, on-prem stacks, edge gateways.
○ Contracts:
§ Resource inventory, status, metrics.
§ Control actions (deployments, scaling, start/stop, config changes).
Integration Patterns & Testing
• Patterns:
○ Request/response, pub/sub, ETL, event sourcing.
• Contract tests:
○ Ensuring type stability, idempotency, error semantics, rate limit handling.
• Observability:
○ Standard metrics/logs for retries, errors, and policy denials.
• Governance:
○ Connector activity is fully visible in Record Auditor & Logbook (7.9).
